Title: PDE-constrained inverse problems at the √(n) rate via debiased physics-informed neural networks

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.12301v1

Published Time: Mon, 14 Sep 2026 00:22:10 GMT

Number of Pages: 91

Markdown Content:
# PDE-constrained inverse problems at the √n rate via debiased physics-informed neural networks 

Yves Atchade and Debarghya Mukherjee Department of Mathematics and Statistics, Boston University 

Abstract 

We study the problem of estimating unknown parameters in PDE-constrained inverse prob-lems from noisy observations, where the PDE solution is approximated using Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs). While PINNs have demonstrated remarkable empirical success, ex-isting estimators often inherit the slow nonparametric convergence rate of the neural-network solution, leading to biased and statistically inefficient inference for the finite-dimensional pa-rameters of interest. To address this, we propose a two-step debiased estimation procedure that combines neural-network-based nonparametric estimation with an influence-function-based bias correction. By eliminating the first-order sensitivity of the estimator to errors in the nuisance function, our procedure yields a √n-consistent and asymptotically normal estimator without requiring undersmoothing of the neural network component. We further extend this framework to Bayesian inference by replacing the original likelihood with a debiased quasi-likelihood and establish a Bernstein–von Mises theorem showing that the resulting posterior contracts at the √n-rate with an asymptotic covariance matching that of the frequentist estimator. As a by-product of our analysis, we establish near-minimax optimal convergence rates for estimating a nonparametric regression function and its derivatives in Sobolev spaces using neural networks. Extensive numerical experiments corroborate our theoretical findings and demonstrate the ne-cessity of the proposed debiasing procedure for valid statistical inference in PDE-constrained inverse problems. 

# 1 Introduction 

Partial differential equations (PDEs) are widely used to describe the evolution of physical quantities over space and time, serving as the foundational modeling framework in many areas of science and engineering [19, 26, 51, 77, 82, 92]. In numerous real-world applications, the general forms of the governing PDEs are known from domain-specific knowledge, but certain key parameters relevant to the underlying physical laws remain unknown. The estimation and inference on these unknown parameters from data is of paramount importance in many applications [31, 45, 47, 48]. For concreteness, consider the steady-state heat-conduction equation 

−∇ · (aθ⋆ (x)∇u⋆(x)) = f (x), x ∈ Ω ,

along with appropriate boundary conditions. Here u⋆ denotes the temperature field, f is the known source function, and aθ⋆ is the thermal conductivity of the material. For simplicity, assume that log aθ⋆ (x) = Pqj=1 θ⋆,j ϕj (x), i.e., it belongs to the span of q (known) functions {ϕj : 1 ≤ j ≤ q}.Given n noisy temperature measurements of the form Yi = u⋆(Xi) + ϵi, the goal is to recover the finite-dimensional conductivity parameter θ⋆. The unknown temperature field u⋆ must also be estimated from the data, and hence acts as an infinite-dimensional nuisance parameter. 1arXiv:2609.12301v1 [math.ST] 11 Sep 2026 Recovering θ∗ from noisy observations in problems of this kind falls under the purview of the inverse problem [2, 8, 14, 15, 20, 38, 41, 50, 61, 66, 68, 69, 88]. Methodologies developed to tackle this from both frequentist and Bayesian perspectives can be divided into two main categories: direct (one-step) methods and indirect (regularized or two-step) methods. The direct approach involves parameterizing the distribution of observations directly in terms of the unknown parameter and the 

forward operator ([15, 16, 66, 68, 70]). While theoretically appealing, implementing this approach is notoriously challenging in practice due to the complexity of the forward operator. For example, computing the gradients needed for gradient-based optimization is computationally demanding, and constructing a surrogate model/optimization problem to bypass this often introduces additional approximation errors in the estimation process that are typically not well understood. To bypass the need to query the forward operator or its derivatives, the indirect approach simplifies the optimization problem by estimating the infinite-dimensional PDE solution and the finite-dimensional parameter via regularization. Although the indirect approach has been stud-ied extensively in the statistical literature (e.g., see [6, 27, 43, 58, 74, 76, 97, 100] and references therein), there has been a recent resurgence of interest in this formulation as researchers have be-gun employing deep neural networks to solve these PDE-constrained optimization problems under the name of Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) [33, 49, 56, 75, 85, 99, 101]. Leverag-ing recent advances in deep learning and automatic differentiation, PINNs provide a flexible and computationally attractive tool for jointly estimating parameters and the PDE solution from noisy observations. Despite their empirical success across various fields, the theoretical understanding of the statistical properties of PINN-based estimators remains limited [30, 60, 89]. A critical limitation of the standard PINN-based approach is that the estimate of the finite-dimensional parameter of interest typically inherits the slower, non-parametric convergence rate from the neural network-based estimation of the PDE solution. As a consequence, the widely used PINN estimators for PDE parameters are typically biased and statistically inefficient. In classical semi-parametric inference, such biases are often mitigated by undersmoothing the non-parametric component (e.g., by selecting a smaller bandwidth in kernel regression or using more knots in spline-based estimators) [12, 39, 42, 43, 59, 63, 72, 94]. However, when employing deep neural networks, it is unclear which parameters (e.g., depth, width, or learning rate) should be carefully tuned to achieve the necessary undersmoothing, especially when fine-tuning pre-trained networks (see [103] for a recent work in that direction). This paper aims to bridge this critical gap by addressing the following question: Is it possible to estimate the unknown parameters of the PDE at a parametric 

√n rate while using PINNs to estimate the solution? 

To answer this, we study PINN-based inverse problems through the lens of the theory of efficient estimation of functionals in semiparametric models [11, 91]. Inspired by the idea of bias correction in double machine learning/Neyman orthogonalization [24, 25, 37, 64, 65], we propose a novel debiasing technique that yields an estimator that does not require the initial neural network estimator to be undersmoothed. Specifically, we develop a simple two-step estimation procedure that combines neural-network-based nonparametric estimation of the solution with an influence-function-based debiasing strategy [46, 62, 78]. By performing a one-step Taylor expansion on the first-order condition of the PDE-based risk functional, we carefully construct a debiased score function whose first-order sensitivity to errors in the nuisance function is eliminated. This bias correction ensures that the effect of the first-stage estimation error enters only at a higher order, yielding a statistically valid, √n-consistent, and asymptotically normal (CAN) estimator of the finite-dimensional PDE parameters. The estimation of the finite-dimensional parameters at the root-n rate in a semiparametric model is also an actively investigated problem in the Bayesian literature [10, 21–23, 79]. By replacing the likelihood with a suitably debiased quasi-likelihood function, we develop a Bayesian extension of our 2debiasing technique. The resulting debiased posterior distribution bears similarities with two recent works on Bayesian posterior correction in semiparametric models. In [102], the authors leverage influence functions and the one-step update estimator to develop a posterior correction method for models with a probability measure nuisance parameter. More closely related to our work is the general Bayesian Neyman orthogonalization framework proposed by [80]. An important limitation of [80] is that the conditions under which their corrected posterior defines a valid probability measure must be verified on a case-by-case basis, and it is unclear whether their framework works in the current setting. By contrast, our debiased posterior distributions are always well-defined and can be readily explored via MCMC, and in instances involving linear PDEs with a Gaussian prior 

π0, exact Monte Carlo sampling is even feasible. On the theory side, we establish a Bernstein–von Mises theorem with a limiting covariance matrix that matches that of the corresponding frequentist estimator, thereby ensuring asymptotic agreement between Bayesian and frequentist inference. In summary, the statistically valid inferential procedures developed in this work address a critical methodological need, enabling researchers across disciplines to draw reliable scientific conclusions when applying modern machine learning tools to PDE-constrained inverse problems. We summarize our contributions below: 1. We develop a novel debiasing approach to obtain a √n-consistent and asymptotically normal (CAN) estimator of the unknown parameters in PDE-constrained models when the underlying solution is approximated using neural networks, such as PINNs. Our methodology is compu-tationally efficient and straightforward to implement. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that provides rigorous √n-rate statistical guarantees for parameter estimation in PDE-constrained inverse problems using flexible machine learning models. 2. As a by-product, we establish near-minimax-optimal convergence rates for neural-network estimators of a nonparametric regression function and its derivatives simultaneously over the larger Sobolev class W β, 2, in contrast to much of the existing neural-network regression theory, which typically assumes more restrictive finite H¨ older-type smoothness [5, 7, 9, 35, 54, 55, 81]. Statistical theory for derivative estimation using neural networks is comparatively scarce. One approach is to smooth a flexible first-stage regression estimator before differentiating it: [36] estimate partial derivatives by convolving a deep neural network regression estimator with a smoothing kernel, while [53] propose a kernel-resmoothing scheme for arbitrary machine-learning regression estimators in the estimation of average partial effects. These approaches, however, do not directly establish simultaneous convergence rates for the derivatives of the same fitted neural-network estimator in Sobolev norms. Perhaps closest in spirit is [32], which studies a semi-supervised deep Sobolev regressor based on ReQU networks and pro-vides guarantees for estimating both the regression function and its gradient; however, their derivative-estimation rate is not minimax optimal. Although simultaneous approximation results for neural networks have been established in H¨ older spaces [7, 29], analogous results in Sobolev spaces, covering both approximation and stochastic errors to yield a statistical guarantee, appear to be missing in the literature. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to establish simultaneous minimax-optimal rates (up to a log factor) for estimating the regression function and its derivatives in Sobolev spaces using neural networks. 3. We further develop a novel Bayesian debiasing framework in which the likelihood function is suitably modified to incorporate the debiased score function. Under a mild log-concavity assumption on the prior distribution we show that the marginal posterior distribution of the parameter θ contracts at the √n rate. Moreover, we establish a Bernstein–von Mises theorem demonstrating that the resulting Bayesian posterior distribution has the same asymptotic 3covariance as the corresponding frequentist estimator, thereby ensuring asymptotic agreement between Bayesian and frequentist inference. Our analysis provides a unified treatment of PDE-constrained parameter estimation when (i) the underlying mean function is estimated via neural networks, and (ii) a suitable debiasing procedure is incorporated to remove the bias arising from nonparametric estimation and achieve √n-consistent inference for the parameter of interest. 4. Finally, we complement our theoretical results with extensive numerical experiments. These studies corroborate our theoretical findings and, in particular, demonstrate the necessity of the proposed debiasing procedure to obtain valid statistical inference for the parameter of interest. The rest of the manuscript is organized as follows. In Section 2, we introduce the problem formu-lation and present our methodology in detail from both frequentist and Bayesian perspectives. In Section 3, we establish the theoretical guarantees of our approach, including √n-consistency and asymptotic normality from the frequentist perspective (Section 3.2), as well as a Bernstein–von Mises theorem from the Bayesian perspective. In Section 4, we present numerical experiments that validate our theoretical results and demonstrate the necessity of the proposed debiasing procedure. Finally, in Section 5, we conclude the paper and discuss several directions for future research. 

# 2 Methodology 

Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded open domain with a sufficiently regular boundary. For ease of exposi-tion, we assume throughout that Ω = (0 , 1) d, although our theoretical framework extends almost verbatim to any bounded domain with smooth (Lipschitz) boundary. Let u⋆ denote an unknown deterministic function that solves the parametrized partial differential equation 

K0u⋆(x) + Ψ θ⋆ (x, K 1u⋆(x)) = 0 , B(u⋆) = 0 , ∀x ∈ Ω. (2.1) Here, K0 is a differential operator of order at most τ (see Section 3 below for precise definition), and K1 = ( K11 , . . . , K 1p)⊤ is a vector of p differential operators, each of order at most τ . The operator B encodes the initial and boundary conditions 1. The parameter of interest θ⋆ ∈ Θ ⊆ Rq

is the unknown finite-dimensional vector that governs the underlying physical law. The function Ψθ(x, K 1u(x)) ≡ Ψ( x, K 1u(x), θ ) with Ψ : Rd × Rp × Rq → R that is assumed to be sufficiently smooth; see Assumption 4 for precise regularity conditions. In a slight abuse of notation we will at times write (Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x) def 

= Ψ θ(x, K 1u(x)) = Ψ( x, K 1u(x), θ ).

Furthermore for any m ∈ { x, z, θ }, we use the notation ∇m(Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x) to denote the partial derivative of Ψ with respect to m evaluated at ( x, K 1(u)( x), θ ), i.e. 

∇m(Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x) = ∇mΨ( x, z, θ )|(x,K 1u(x),θ ) .

The higher-order derivatives are also defined analogously. An experimenter observes noisy measurements of the solution u⋆ at random design points. Specifically, the observed data consist of Dn = {(Xi, Y i)}ni=1 , where Xi ∈ Ω are sampled indepen-dently from a known design distribution p0 supported on Ω (that we take without loss of generality      

> 1The initial and boundary conditions are required for u⋆to be well-defined. However, since Bdoes not depend on
> θ(the main quantity of interest in our setup), it will be ignored in our analysis.

4to be the uniform distribution), and Yi = u⋆(Xi) + ϵi, with {ϵ}ni=1 being independent sub-Gaussian noise variables. Our setting is thus closer in spirit to experimental design rather than observational studies, as the design distribution p0 is assumed to be known. While our framework can be ex-tended to the case of unknown design distribution by estimating p0 from the data, we leave this extension as an interesting future avenue to explore. Our primary objective is to construct a √n-CAN estimator of θ⋆ based on the observed data Dn.The function u⋆ serves as an infinite-dimensional nuisance parameter. In our proposed method, we estimate it using flexible function classes, such as deep neural networks, while carefully correcting for the resulting bias in the estimation of θ⋆. To motivate our proposed methodology, we first describe a standard PINN-based approach and explain why it may fail to achieve valid inference for θ⋆. Define the PDE-based risk functional 

R(θ, u ) = 12

{K0u(x) + (Ψ θ ◦ K1u) ( x)}2 ω(x) dx, (2.2) where ω(x) is a strictly positive weight function on (0 , 1) d that smoothly decays to zero near the boundary; see Assumption 5 for concrete examples. Under some minimal identifiability assumptions (see Assumption 3) R(θ, u ⋆) is uniquely minimized at θ = θ⋆, and in particular, R(θ⋆, u ⋆) = 0 (immediately follows from Equation (2.1)). A standard PINN approach [49, 75, 89, 101] estimates (θ⋆, u ⋆) jointly by solving the penalized optimization problem (ˆ uPINN , ˆθPINN ) = argmin

(Yi − u(Xi)) 2 + λn, 1R(θ, u ) + λn, 2ρn(u) (2.3) where FNN denotes a class of neural networks with prescribed architecture (e.g., depth, width, sparsity), and ρn(u) is a regularization term on the network parameters, such as weight decay. In essence, PINNs solve a nonparametric regression problem augmented with a PDE-based penalty, that encourages the estimated solution to satisfy the governing equation, while estimating θ and u

jointly via gradient-based optimization. Although the standard PINN approach is widely used, largely because of its computational convenience, it has an important limitation for inference on θ⋆. In particular, the resulting estima-tor ˆθPINN typically inherits the nonparametric estimation error of the first-stage solution estimator ˆuPINN [89]. Consequently, even though ˆθPINN is consistent, it generally converges more slowly than the parametric √n-rate and may exhibit substantial finite-sample bias. Moreover, its lim-iting distribution is typically unavailable, precluding the direct construction of theoretically valid analytical confidence intervals. To the best of our knowledge, there is also no general theory estab-lishing the validity of bootstrap- or Monte Carlo-based uncertainty quantification procedures for standard PINN estimators in this setting. In many scientific and physical applications, however, reliable inference on θ⋆ is the primary objective. This motivates the development of a principled debiasing procedure that removes the first-order effect of the estimation error in ˆ uPINN yields a √n-consistent and asymptotically normal estimate of θ∗ while remains computationally straightforward to implement. 

Remark 1. In the preceding formulation, we defined the PDE penalty R(θ, u ) at the population level , involving integration over the spatial domain Ω. In practice, this integral can be approximated using Monte Carlo integration by drawing samples over Ω, or via other standard numerical integra-tion techniques. However, under mild regularity conditions, the resulting numerical approximation error is typically negligible compared to the statistical error arising from the squared loss term, and hence does not affect the asymptotic rate of estimation of (u⋆, θ ⋆). For clarity of exposition, we therefore work directly with the population-level penalty throughout the paper. 

5Remark 2. It is worth pointing out that our framework and results extend verbatim to PDEs of the form K0(u)( x) + (Ψ θ ◦ K1(u))( x) = F (x) for some known source function F , by redefining the function Ψθ(x, z ) as Ψθ(x, z ) − F (x). We focus on the case F ≡ 0 for ease of exposition. 

2.1 Debiased estimation of θ⋆

In this subsection, we present our methodology for constructing a √n-CAN estimator of the PDE parameter θ⋆. As elaborated above, the PINN-based estimator ˆθPINN typically inherits bias from the estimation of the non-parametric function u∗ and consequently converges at a slower rate, lead-ing to a sub-optimal estimator of θ⋆. Our proposed method overcomes this barrier by carefully constructing a debiased estimating equation whose first-order sensitivity to the estimation error of u⋆ is (approximately) eliminated. Consequently, the first-stage error in estimating u⋆ affects inference on θ⋆ only through higher-order terms, allowing the resulting estimator to attain the parametric √n rate. The entire procedure is summarized in Algorithm 1. At a high level, our method consists of two main steps. In the first step, we obtain a consistent preliminary estimator of θ⋆ together with a “good enough”(to be elaborated in Step I below) estimator of the nuisance function u⋆. In the second step, we construct and optimize an appropriate debiased score function, using these first-stage estimators, to obtain our final √n-CAN estimator of θ⋆. We now describe each step in detail. 

Stage I: The objective of the first step is twofold. First, we aim to obtain an initial estima-tor ˆθPI of θ⋆ (defined precisely in Equation (2.5)) that is merely required to be consistent; its rate of convergence may be slow (e.g., nonparametric rates). Second, we require an estimator ˆ u of the PDE solution u⋆ that recovers both u⋆ and its derivatives at the minimax-optimal rate, up to logarithmic factors, with respect to the smoothness parameter β and the dimension d of the do-main. One may opt to use the joint PINN estimator (ˆ uPINN , ˆθPINN ), as defined in Equation (2.3). However, below we propose a simpler alternative that is not only computationally more efficient but also more amenable to theoretical analysis . Specifically, we estimate u⋆ by regressing Y on X

using a class of deep neural networks FNN (see Assumption 2 for details on the architecture). While existing theory for neural networks (e.g., [5, 54, 55, 81]) establishes minimax-optimal convergence rates (up to logarithmic factors) for estimating smooth or compositional functions, it does not im-mediately yield corresponding guarantees for estimating derivatives of the regression function. To address this issue, we propose estimating u⋆ by solving the penalized optimization problem ˆu = argmin

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result establishing that a neural network-based estimator can simultaneously estimate a nonparametric regression function and its derivatives at minimax-optimal rates (up to logarithmic factors). However, from a practical standpoint, our two-step approach is simpler to implement and facilitates a more transparent theoretical analysis. For this reason, we adopt it as our primary procedure. 

Remark 3. At this point a natural question is whether the additional regularization R(u) in the PINN estimator in Equation (2.6) improves the rate of convergence. Observe that the true solution 

u⋆ satisfies R(u⋆) = 0 . Indeed, the subset 

U0 = {u ∈ W β, 2(Ω) : R(u) = 0 }

consists precisely of those functions u that solve the underlying PDE for some θ ∈ Θ. As shown in our previous work [89], under a mild injectivity condition on the forward map (which maps θ′ to 

uθ′ , the solution of the PDE with parameter θ = θ′), the set U0 forms essentially a q-dimensional manifold of W β, 2(Ω) , where q = dim( θ). Moreover, it was demonstrated in [89] that, under suitable H¨ older-type smoothness conditions on the forward map, one can estimate u⋆ at a near-parametric rate if the function class F is restricted to U0.Although such an approach is computationally intractable in practice, this observation raises the natural question of whether the additional penalty R(u) in Equation (2.6) can improve the estimation rate of ˆuPINN by encouraging solutions to concentrate near the low-dimensional manifold 

U0. We conjecture that, under standard sieve-based implementations such as neural network classes, the inherent approximation error prevents the PINN penalty from improving the leading-order rate for estimating u⋆. Since our primary objective is inference on θ⋆, we leave a sharper analysis of possible rate improvements for ˆu as an interesting direction for future work. 

Stage II: A key concept underlying our debiasing strategy is Neyman orthogonality [24, 37, 64, 65], which we illustrate briefly here for the ease of the readers. Let ℓ(θ, η ) denote a loss function depending on a finite-dimensional parameter of interest θ and an infinite-dimensional nuisance 7parameter η and ( θ⋆, η ⋆) denotes the minimizer of the loss function. Let s(θ, η ) = ∇θℓ(θ, η ) be the associated score function. We say that s satisfies Neyman orthogonality at ( θ⋆, η ⋆) if the Gateaux derivative of s(θ⋆, η ) with respect to η vanishes (or more generally, is of order o(n−1/2)) at η = η⋆,that is, 

∂∂t s θ⋆, η ⋆ + th 

> t=0

= 0 ,

for all h in a shrinking neighborhood around η⋆ with respect to n. Under standard regularity conditions, this orthogonality property ensures that the estimator of θ obtained by solving the empirical version of the equation s(θ, ˆη) = 0 is √n-consistent and asymptotically normal, provided the nuisance estimator ˆ η converges to η⋆ at a rate faster than n−1/4. Intuitively, Neyman orthog-onality essentially removes the first-order impact of nuisance estimation error, so that the leading asymptotic behavior of the estimator for θ is unaffected by replacing η⋆ with ˆ η.One of the main contribution of this work is the construction of an (approximately) Neyman-orthogonal score function associated with the PDE risk functional R(θ, u ) defined in Equation (2.2). The score function corresponding to R(θ, u ) is given by (∇θR)( θ, u ) =

2.2 Debiased Bayesian inference 

A Bayesian PINN formulation of the PDE parameter estimation learns ( θ, u ) jointly. Following [101] and the setup studied in [89], let ¯Π0 be a given joint prior distribution on Rq × W β, 2(Ω). First, we turn ¯Π0 into the so-called PINN prior by considering the probability measure with density proportional to ( θ, u ) 7 → e−λR(θ,u )/2 ¯Π(d θ, du), where λ > 0 is a regularization parameter. Here, the term λR(θ, u )/2 is used to enforce the PDE structure. Using this PINN prior distribution and the data distribution, the joint PINN posterior distribution of ( θ, u ) on Rq × W β, 2(Ω) is given by Π(d θ, du|D n) ∝ exp − 12σ2

> n

X

> i=1

(Yi − u(Xi)) 2 − λ

2 R(θ, u )

!

¯Π0(d θ, du). (2.10) This posterior distribution has been recently studied in [89] where the authors show that the marginal posterior distribution of u contracts at the minimax nonparametric rate, and the marginal posterior of θ contracts at the corresponding plug-in rate. Let Π (θ)(·|D n) (resp. Π(θ|u)(·|D n), and Π (u)(·|D n)) denote the marginal posterior distribution of θ (resp. the conditional posterior distribution of θ given u, and the marginal posterior distribution of u) under the joint distribution in Equation (2.10). We can re-express the marginal posterior distribution of θ as Π(θ)(d θ|D n) = 

Z 

> Wβ, 2(Ω)

Π(θ|u)(d θ|u, Dn) Π (u)(d u|D n),

which shows that indeed, as in the frequentist setting, Bayesian inference for θ is a plug-in method that suffers from the same slow convergence rate in estimating u. This raises the question: is it possible to correct the bias and achieve the √n-estimation rate in the Bayesian estimation of θ?

The estimation of finite-dimensional parameters at the √n-rate in semiparametric models has been actively investigated in the Bayesian literature [10, 21–23, 79]. As in the frequentist setting, a commonly used approach to achieve the √n-rate relies on the local asymptotic normality (LAN) expansion of the log-likelihood together with careful tuning of the prior distribution to control bias (see, e.g., [79] for a survey). And as pointed out above, it is unclear how this approach applies in settings such as ours, where the exact bias-variance trade-off of deep neural network-based models is not fully understood. We extend our debiasing technique into a novel approach for debiasing posterior distributions. Let π0 be a prior density on Rq with respect to the Lebesgue measure (it need not coincide with the θ-marginal of ¯Π0 introduced above). Given a symmetric positive definite matrix Vn ∈ Rq×q,9and using the de-biased score function Sdb  

> n

(Equation (2.8)), we consider the following debiased conditional posterior distribution of θ given u given by Π(θ|u)db (d θ|u, Dn) ∝ exp 



− n

2 Sdb  

> n

(θ, u )⊤V −1 

> n

Sdb  

> n

(θ, u )



π0(θ)d θ. (2.11) Given an initial estimator ˆ u of u⋆, our proposed debiased Bayesian approach consists in drawing inference on θ using the (plug-in) probability measure Π (θ|u)db (·| ˆu, Dn). A fully Bayesian flavor of this procedure can also be defined by employing an initial marginal posterior distribution of u (such as the u-marginal obtained from Equation (2.10)), leading to the alternative de-biased posterior Π(θ)db (d θ|D n) = 

Z 

> Wβ, 2(Ω)

Π(θ|u)db (d θ|u, Dn) Π (u)(d u|D n). (2.12) One important limitation of (2.12) is that it can be substantially more challenging to handle com-putationally than (2.11). Hence, out of computational considerations, we will focus our analysis on the plug-in posterior Π (θ|u)db (·| ˆu, Dn). However, provided that the marginal posterior Π (u)(·|D n)employed has contraction properties similar to those of ˆ u, our results extend readily to (2.12). A summary of the resulting debiased Bayesian inference is given in Algorithm 2. Regarding Vn,the debiased posterior can be constructed with any positive definite matrix Vn, however a careful choice of Vn is needed for the resulting posterior inference to match the frequentist inference, and we address this question in the theoretical discussions. 

Algorithm 2 Bayesian two-step debiased estimation of θ⋆

Input: Data {(Xi, Y i)}ni=1 , the PDE map, a collection of DNNs {u, u ∈ F NN } and, if available, a symmetric positive definite matrix Vn.

Output: A rate optimal estimator ˆ u of u⋆, and posterior samples {θ(0) , . . . , θ (K)}. 

> 1:

Obtain ˆ u by solving the minimization problem Equation (2.4) over FNN to obtain ˆ u. 

> 2:

Compute ˆθPI = argmin θ R(θ, ˆu) as in Equation (2.5).  

> 3:

If needed, use (ˆ u, ˆθPI ) to compute the weight matrix Vn. 

> 4:

Use MCMC to produce sample {θ(0) , . . . , θ (K)} from the debiased posterior distribution Π(θ|u)db (·| ˆu, Dn) as defined in (2.11).  

> 5:

Return ˆ u and {θ(0) , . . . , θ (K)}.

Before stating the main theorems, we first introduce the assumptions required for their proofs. 

Assumption 1 (Data generating process) . The design points {Xi}1≤i≤n are assumed to be gen-erated uniformly from Ω = (0 , 1) d. The responses are generated as Yi = u⋆(Xi) + ϵi, where 

u⋆ ∈ W β, 2(Ω , L ) in the classical sense and satisfy the PDE equation (2.1) for some θ = θ⋆ and the smoothness index satisfy β − 2τ > d , where τ ≥ 1 is the maximum order of the derivatives in the PDE equation. The errors {ϵi}1≤i≤n are i.i.d. subgaussian random variables with mean 0 and variance σ2 

> ϵ

and are independent of Xi.

Assumption 1 represents a standard setup in nonparametric statistical learning. The compact support condition on Ω and the sub-Gaussian assumption on ϵ can, in principle, be relaxed at the cost of additional technical arguments, without altering the fundamental statistical insights of the problem. We therefore adopt these conventional assumptions to streamline the presentation. 

Remark 5. For the debiasing step and to establish asymptotic normality of the debiased estimator 

ˆθdb , we impose the condition β − 2τ > d . If instead we assume u⋆ ∈ Cβ,γ (Ω , L ), that is, u⋆ belongs to a H¨ older class, then the corresponding requirement would reduce to β − 2τ > d/ 2. At present, it is not clear whether these smoothness conditions are optimal. Investigating their sharpness remains an important direction for future work. 

Next, we describe the architecture of the neural network collection. We will use a standard RePU( ν) activation function (with ν ≥ β) with width N , depth L, and total number of trainable weights W . To keep the neural network outputs bounded as well as smooth, we apply a smooth truncation. In particular, let Tm ∈ Cν∗ (ν∗ ≥ β) be a piecewise polynomial function that smoothly truncates x at level m, i.e. Tm(x) = x for all |x| ≤ m − δ and Tm(x) = sign (x)m for |x| ≥ m,where m is a large constant (typically one may take m > 2∥u⋆∥∞). See Lemma S.9.1 of the supplementary document for the exact definition. We slightly enlarge our collection FNN as ˜FNN =

{Tm ◦ u : u}, where u is a RePU( ν) network with width N depth L and total number of parameters 12 W . Assuming that neural network outputs are truncated or uniformly bounded is standard in the theory of neural networks (e.g., see Equation (4) of [81], or the definition of Tβ in [55] or the definition of ¯Tm in Definition 2 of [35]). Traditionally, this is achieved by the hard truncation map 

T (x) = sign (x)( |x| ∧ m). However, this map is non-smooth, and hence the derivatives of functions in FNN are not well defined at the truncation points. Since our goal is to estimate both the target function and its derivatives simultaneously, we use a smooth truncation scheme instead. Because 

ν ≥ β, the resulting truncated neural networks continue to belong to the Sobolev space W β, 2.

Assumption 2 (Function class FNN ). We choose FNN as a collection of feedforward neural network functions Ω → R, with width of the order N = c1(n/ log n)d/ (2 β+d), depth of the order c2, and total number of weights of the order c3(n/ log n)d/ (2 β+d), along with RePU( ν) activation function for some ν ≥ β, where c1, c 2, c 3 are constants independent of n. Furthermore, we set ˜FNN = {Tm ◦ u :

u ∈ F NN }.

Remark 6. For large values of ν, the RePU( ν) activation function is known to be numerically unstable, and the modified rectified power unit (mRePU) has been proposed as an alternative [52]. The mRePU( ν) is defined as mRePU ν (z) = z(z + 1) ν if z ≥ − 1, and zero otherwise, which can be written as the difference of two RePU: mRePU ν (z) = RePU ν+1 (z + 1) − RePU ν (z + 1) . Therefore our results directly apply to mRePU activation functions as well. 

Assumption 3 (Local identifiability and curvature) . Assume that Θ ⊂ Rq is compact and that 

θ⋆ ∈ int(Θ) . Moreover, assume the following. (i) Forward well-posedness and local injectivity. There exists a neighborhood Nθ of θ⋆ such that, for every θ ∈ N θ, the PDE system K0(u) + Ψ θ ◦ K1(u) = 0 along with B(u) = 0 , admits a unique solution u(θ) ∈ W β, 2(Ω) . The forward map θ 7 → u(θ) is locally injective at θ⋆ and continuous at θ⋆ in the W 2τ, 2 norm. (ii) Identification of the inverse problem. The true parameter θ⋆ is the unique minimizer of θ 7 → R (θ, u ⋆) over Θ. Equivalently, for every η > 0,

inf   

> θ∈Θ: ∥θ−θ⋆∥2≥η

R(θ, u ⋆) > 0.

(iii) Local curvature. There exist constants δ0, δ 1, λ − > 0 such that B(θ⋆, δ 1) ⊂ Θ and 

inf   

> ∥u−u⋆∥W2τ, 2≤δ0

inf 

> ∥θ−θ⋆∥2≤δ1

∇2

> θ

R(θ, u ) ≥ λ−.

Assumption 3 consists of three mild requirements: local well-posedness of the forward problem, identifiability of the inverse problem, and local curvature of the PDE risk functional. Recall that 

B encodes the boundary and/or initial conditions needed to make the PDE solution well defined, while R(θ, u ) denotes the weighted L2 norm of the interior PDE residual. Since the weight function 

w is strictly positive in the interior of Ω (see Assumption 5), the condition R(θ, u ) = 0 implies that the PDE residual vanishes almost everywhere on Ω. The first condition states that for every θ in a neighborhood of θ⋆, the PDE admits a unique solution u(θ) ∈ W β, 2(Ω). Thus the forward map 

θ 7 → u(θ) is locally well defined around θ⋆. The additional local injectivity and continuity of this map around θ⋆ are standard stability requirements in inverse problems: nearby parameters produce nearby solutions, and no two nearby parameter values generate the same solution [67, 88]. The second requirement is an identifiability condition for the inverse problem. Since our pre-liminary estimator ˆθPI is defined as a minimizer of θ 7 → R (θ, ˆu) over Θ, it is not enough to assume 13 only local injectivity of the forward map. We also require that θ⋆ be the unique minimizer of the population risk θ 7 → R (θ, u ⋆) over Θ. This condition rules out distant parameters that fit the true solution equally well and ensures consistency of the plug-in estimator ˆθPI as long as ˆ u is a consistent estimate of u⋆.The third requirement imposes a local curvature condition on the PDE risk functional. Specif-ically, it requires that the Hessian ∇2

> θ

R(θ, u ) remain uniformly positive definite for ( θ, u ) in a neighborhood of ( θ⋆, u ⋆). This is a mild local nondegeneracy condition on the sensitivity of the PDE residual with respect to θ, analogous to the positive-definiteness of the Fisher information matrix in classical parametric inference. It guarantees that the risk is locally strongly convex in θ,so that perturbations of the solution u⋆ lead only to stable perturbations of the local minimizer in 

θ. Assumption 3 is satisfied by many standard parametric PDE models (see Section 4 and Section S.1 of the supplementary document for some illustrative examples). 

Assumption 4 (Smoothness and local boundedness of Ψ) . Let Θ0 ⊆ Rq be an open convex set containing the parameter space Θ, and let O ⊆ Rd be an open set such that Ω ⊆ O . The nonlinear map 

Ψ : O × Rp × Θ0 → R, (x, z, θ ) 7 → Ψ( x, z, θ ) = Ψ θ(x, z ),

is sufficiently smooth in the spatial variable x, the feature variable z, and the parameter θ. Specif-ically,, for multi-indices a ∈ Nq

Assumption 4 imposes standard regularity and growth conditions on the nonlinear map Ψ θ,ensuring that the PDE residual and the associated risk functional are sufficiently smooth in both the parameter θ and the state variable. The requirement that θ 7 → Ψθ(x) be thrice continuously differentiable guarantees that the PDE risk R(θ, u ) admits well-defined second- and third-order derivatives in θ, which are required for establishing local curvature, performing Taylor expansions, and deriving asymptotic normality of the debiased estimator. Importantly, these conditions are mild and standard in nonlinear PDE-constrained estimation and semiparametric inference: they merely ensure controlled growth and local smoothness, rather than imposing restrictive structural assumptions on Ψ θ.

Assumption 5 (Weight function) . The weight function ω belongs to Cβc ( ¯Ω) , i.e., 

sup 

> |α|≤ β

∥∂αω∥∞ < ∞ and ∂αω(x) = 0 ∀ | α| ≤ β, ∀ x ∈ ∂Ω ,

and ω(x) > 0 for all x ∈ Ω. Since Ω = (0 , 1) d, one such example is ω(x) = Π dj=1 [xj (1 − xj )] (β+1) .

14 Assumption 5 ensures that the weight function is sufficiently smooth and vanishes at the bound-ary with high enough order to eliminate boundary terms arising from integration by parts up to order 2 τ . The positivity of w in the interior guarantees that the PDE residual is meaningfully penalized throughout Ω, preserving identifiability.

Having established this proposition, we are now in a position to present our main results from both the frequentist and Bayesian perspectives. 15 3.2 Frequentist approach 

Recall that in our frequentist framework, we tackle the problem in two stages. In the first stage, we estimate the function u by solving Equation (2.4) using neural networks, and based on the resulting estimator ˆ u, we construct an initial plug-in estimator ˆθPI (see Equation (2.5)). In the second stage, we introduce a debiased score (or loss) function Sdb  

> n

(θ, ˆu) (Equation (2.8)), and define our final estimator ˆθdb as the minimizer of its norm over a shrinking parameter set Θ n, where Θ n is chosen as a neighborhood of ˆθPI . As per the chronology, in our first result, we show that we can estimate 

u⋆ and its derivatives at a minimax optimal rate (up to a log factor) using RePU neural networks. The proof is given in Section S.4 of the supplementary document. 

Theorem 3.5. Consider the estimation of u via solving Equation (2.4) with FNN replaced by ˜FNN 

(Assumption 2) and λn ≍ (n/ log n)−2β/ (2 β+d) log n. Then, under Assumption 1, the estimator ˆu

satisfies with probability ≥ 1 − n−C for some large C > 0:

∥ˆu∥W β, 2 ≤ 8∥u⋆∥W β, 2 + K1 ≤ 8L + K1, for some K1 > 0,

∥∂α ˆu − ∂αu⋆∥L2(PX ) ≤ K2 (log n) 12  

> 
> 1−|α|
> β
> 

 n

log n

− β−| α|

> 2β+d

, ∀ 0 ≤ | α| < β . 

Remark 8. The above theorem establishes the rate of convergence of a neural network–based esti-mator of u⋆ and its derivatives obtained by minimizing a penalized squared error loss (see (2.4) ). We highlight several aspects of this result: 

• To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that analyzes a neural network estimator constructed via a general Sobolev-penalized squared loss and proves that both the mean func-tion and its derivatives can be estimated simultaneously at the minimax optimal rate [87]. The presence of a Sobolev penalty distinguishes our approach from standard DNN-based re-gression analyses (e.g., [5, 9, 35, 54, 55, 81] to name a few) and requires different technical arguments. The most closely related work is [32], which incorporates a first-order derivative penalty. However, that work does not establish minimax optimal rates for derivative esti-mation. Moreover, [32] focuses on the ReQU network framework as they only penalize the gradient, whereas we consider a general RePU( ν) network with ν ≥ β to ensure that the estimator lies in W β, 2(Ω) . Furthermore, in [32], the authors assumed the mean function be-longs to a H¨ older class, whereas we assume they belong to a Sobolev class, which substantially broadens the scope of the result. 

• Our analysis relies on a new approximation theorem for Sobolev spaces using RePU( ν) net-works (Theorem S.8.1 of the supplementary document). Although approximation results for RePU networks exist in the literature (e.g., [57, 84]), those typically require the network depth to grow with n, which introduces additional logarithmic factors in the convergence rate. In contrast, our approximation result allows the depth to remain fixed while only the width and the total number of active parameters increase with n at the optimal rate. 

• Finally, much of the existing neural network literature assumes the regression function be-longs to a H¨ older class. Working under a Sobolev smoothness assumption presents additional analytical challenges. In particular, available approximation results for Sobolev functions are typically established in the L2 norm, rather than the L∞ norm commonly used in the H¨ older setting, which further complicates the analysis. 

16 The previous theorem establishes the convergence rate of ˆ u, defined in Equation (2.4). The following corollary provides an analogous result for ˆ uPINN , as defined in Equation (2.6). The proof is provided in Section S.4 of the supplementary document. 

Corollary 3.6. Let ˆuPINN denote the estimator of u⋆ obtained from Equation (2.6) , with eFNN .Assume the conditions of Theorem 3.5 along with Assumptions 4 and 5. Suppose

We now turn to our next theorem, which concerns the initial plug-in estimator ˆθPI defined in Equation (2.5). We show that ˆθPI is consistent and attains at least a nonparametric rate of convergence. The proof is given in Section S.4 of the supplementary document. 

Lemma 3.7 (Consistency of ˆθPI ). Let ˆθPI be the initial estimator as defined in Equation (2.5) .Then under Assumption 1-5 and Θ is a compact convex subset of Rq, we have:

The above lemma guarantees that the plug-in estimator ˆθPI consistently estimates the target parameter θ⋆. Its convergence rate is at least of nonparametric order and, more precisely, is driven by the estimation rate of the τ -th derivative of u⋆, since the PDE constraint linking u and θ (Equa-tion (2.1)) involves differential operators of order at most τ . Notably, this rate essentially coincides with the posterior contraction rates established in [89, Theorem 3.1]. However, as elaborated in Section 2, we do not require rate optimality for ˆθPI in the present analysis. Our objective is merely to construct a shrinking neighborhood around θ⋆ to facilitate the subsequent debiasing step. In this regard, the (potentially slow) nonparametric rate is sufficient for our purposes. We now turn to the debiasing step. It is evident from the closed-form expression of the represen-ter Gθ,u (Proposition 3.4) that it depends on derivatives of x 7 → Ψθ(x, K 1(u)( x)) up to order τ , and consequently involves derivatives of u up to order 2 τ . This explains the requirement β − 2τ > d 

in our analysis, which ensures that these higher-order derivatives can be estimated consistently, thereby allowing consistent estimation of the representer. As discussed in Remark 5, if instead we assume u⋆ ∈ Cβ (Ω), then the weaker condition β − 2τ > d/ 2 would suffice. This corresponds to a standard first-order condition in double/debiased machine learning (DML) [24, 25, 37], ensuring that the 2 τ -th derivative of u belongs to a Donsker class. The following result is the first key result of the paper, which states that under the above-mentioned assumption, the debiased estimator ˆθdb 

is indeed √n-CAN. The proof is given in Section 6. 

Theorem 3.8. Let ˆθdb be the debiased estimator, obtained via Algorithm 1. Then under Assump-tion 1-5 and Θ is a compact convex subset of Rq, the estimator ˆθdb satisfies:

The limiting variance of ˆθdb admits a sandwich form, involving both the Hessian of R(θ, u )with respect to θ and the variance of Gθ⋆,u ⋆ . Such a variance structure is common in nuisance-adjusted estimation problems and in pseudo-likelihood-based procedures (e.g., see Theorems 3.1 and 3.3. of [24] for a general result). However, we cannot immediately claim that this variance is semiparametrically efficient, even under Gaussian noise. To see this, note that one may alternatively 17 consider a direct estimator based on the forward map operator Γ : Θ → W β, 2(Ω), which maps 

θ → u(θ), the solution of the PDE Equation (2.1) [70]. Under Gaussian noise, one may then define ˆθ = argmin 

> θ∈Θ

 Yi − Γ( θ)( Xi)2,

which coincides with the maximum likelihood estimator and, under suitable smoothness conditions on Γ, would be expected to achieve semiparametric efficiency. Nevertheless, this approach has two major limitations: (i) the estimator is computationally intractable in practice, as it requires repeatedly solving a nonlinear PDE for each θ, and (ii) the regularity conditions required on the forward map Γ to establish asymptotic efficiency may be substantially stronger than those imposed in our analysis of ˆθdb . Consequently, it is not immediately clear whether the limiting variance of ˆθdb is directly comparable to that of the likelihood-based estimator. We leave establishing such a comparison as an interesting direction for future work. 

Consistent estimation of the limiting variance. To draw inference on θ⋆, we need to con-struct a consistent estimator of the limiting variance of ˆθdb . From the smoothness of ∇2R(θ, u )(see Assumption 4), one may use ˆM = ∇2

3.3 Bayesian theoretical results 

As with our frequentist estimator, we show here that by plug-in of the first stage estimator ˆ u,the debiased posterior distribution Π (θ|u)db (·| ˆu, Dn) contracts around θ⋆ at the √n-rate under similar regularity assumptions. We assume that the prior π0 is strongly log-concave. Specifically: 

Assumption 6. 1. log π0 is of class C1 on Rq, and there exist constants 0 < v ≤ ¯v such that for all θ, θ ′ ∈ Rq,

for some v0 > 0, which satisfies Assumption 6 with v = ¯ v = v0. The shrinking variance 1/(v0 log( n)) 

plays a similar role as the shrinking neighborhood Θn used in the frequentist approach and focuses the posterior mass on compact neighborhoods of θ⋆. In fact, we show in Lemma S.6.3 of the supplementary document that under Assumption 6, the debiased posterior distribution concentrates on balls of radius c√q around θ⋆ for some constant c. # 4 Numerical Experiments 

In this section, we present a simulation study to investigate the finite-sample performance of the estimators introduced in Section 2, with particular emphasis on illustrating the necessity of the proposed debiasing step for valid inference on the PDE parameter. We consider a one–dimensional elliptic diffusion equation: 

−∇ · (aθ(x)∇u(x)) + θ3u(x) = f (x), x ∈ (0 , 1) ,

with boundary conditions u(0) = u(1) = 0, where the function aθ is parametric of the form 

aθ(x) = exp (θ1 cos( πx ) + θ2 cos(2 πx )) .

The source function f is assumed known, and taken in this example as f (x) = 5 .0 + 2 .5 sin(3 .0πx ). This equation is a canonical model for a wide range of physical phenomena in which an unknown medium property must be recovered from indirect measurements. For instance, in thermal con-ductivity estimation, u is the temperature field of a material with location-dependent thermal conductivity aθ. This PDE falls in our general setting with θ = ( θ1, θ 2, θ 3)⊤,

K0 = 0 , K1 = ( I, ∂ x, ∂ xx )⊤, and Ψθ(x, (z1, z 2, z 3)) = θ3z1 − a′

> θ

(x)z2 − aθ(x)z3 − f (x),

which is infinitely differentiable with bounded derivatives of any compact set. Hence, Assumption 4 holds. 

4.1 Data-generating process 

The forward solution uθ has no closed form and is approximated by the finite element method (piecewise-linear elements on a uniform mesh of 4000 elements, see e.g. [17]). We set the true parameter to θ⋆ = (0 .5, 0.3, 2.0) ⊤ and generate observations as follows: 1. generate Xi i.i.d. 

∼ Unif(0 , 1), 1 ≤ i ≤ n;2. set Yi = uθ⋆ (Xi) + εi, with εi i.i.d. 

∼ N (0 , σ 2), σ = 0 .02. We report results for two sample sizes, n ∈ { 500 , 5000 }.20 4.2 Implementation of the first-stage estimators 

Following Section 2.1 we define the PDE risk with the boundary-vanishing weight ω(x) = [ x(1 −x)] 2,

R(θ, u ) = 12

Z 10

ω(x) rθ(x)2 dx, rθ = θ3u −  aθu′′ − f. (4.1) Throughout the example u⋆ is approximated by a fully connected neural network with scalar input, three hidden layers of width 64, and scalar output, using the modified rectified power unit activation of order k = 5, mRePU k, which is Ck−1 = C4 and hence guarantees continuity of u through its fourth-order derivative — required for the representer Gθ,u . Derivatives are evaluated by automatic differentiation in single precision. The Sobolev penalty and the expectations defining R(θ, u ) are approximated by Monte Carlo collocation using 4000 points drawn uniformly on (0 , 1). To estimate 

bu we minimise the empirical squared loss augmented with a W 4,2 Sobolev penalty (on derivatives up to order four) and a Dirichlet boundary penalty enforcing u(0) = u(1) = 0; the Sobolev weight is selected by cross-validation on the PDE residual separately for each sample size, giving λsob =10 −11 × (n/ 500) −10 /11 . The PINN estimator adds the profiled PDE penalty with weight λpde = 0 .1. Networks are trained with Adam (learning rate 2 × 10 −3 for bu, 10 −3 for the PINN objective) with global-norm gradient clipping, for 10 ,000 epochs, with early stopping (patience 1000) for the PINN estimator. The plug-in estimator bθPI is obtained by solving this nonlinear least-squares problem with u replaced by the first-stage estimator bu, using a trust-region, initialised at θ = (0 , 0, 0) ⊤.

4.4 Monte Carlo design and results 

We repeat the experiment over B = 100 independent Monte Carlo runs for each sample size. In every run we record the four estimators bθPINN , bθPI , bθdb , and the debiased posterior mean. The 21 results are displayed on Figure 1-2. The biases and the coverages probabilities are also given in Table 1-2. It is clear that both bθPI and bθPINN exhibit substantial bias in all coordinates, that is markedly reduced by the debiased estimator bθdb . The asymptotic normality of bθdb is supported by the Q–Q plots, and its 95% intervals attain approximately nominal coverage. The middle columns in Figure 1-2 show the output from one MCMC run from the posterior distribution. The result is approximately Gaussian and close to the Bernstein-von Mises limit (in dashed line).

Columns: boxplots of bθPINN , bθPI , bθdb and the posterior mean; debiased posterior density from a single run with the BvM limit, the estimators and the true value overlaid; Q–Q plot of √n(bθdb − θ⋆) against N (0 , 1).

# 5 Conclusion and future work 

In this paper, we developed a debiasing framework for statistical inference in PDE-constrained inverse problems when the unknown PDE solution is estimated using flexible neural-network meth-ods such as PINNs. Standard plug-in or PINN-based estimators of the finite-dimensional PDE parameter generally inherit the slower nonparametric convergence rate of the estimated solution, leading to biased and inefficient inference. To overcome this difficulty, we constructed an influence-function-based debiased score whose first-order sensitivity to errors in the nuisance function is eliminated. The resulting estimator is √n-consistent and asymptotically normal without requiring undersmoothing of the neural-network estimator. We also proposed a Bayesian analogue based on a debiased quasi-likelihood and established a Bernstein-von Mises theorem showing that the Bayesian inference based on debiased posterior distribution agrees asymptotically with the corre-24 Figure 3: Diffusion PDE example with n = 500. We report the estimation bias ( √n × Bias) of the plug-in estimator ˆθPI , the debiased estimator ˆθdb and the debiased posterior mean across the three parameters ( θ1, θ 2, θ 3), for different values of the regularization parameter λsob . Based on B = 100 replications. sponding frequentist inference. As a by-product, we obtained near-minimax-optimal convergence rates for estimating a Sobolev-smooth regression function and its derivatives simultaneously using neural networks, which may be of independent interest. However, several interesting directions remain open for future investigation. First, although our debiased estimator attains the parametric rate, it is not yet clear whether its limiting variance is semiparametrically efficient. A natural benchmark is the likelihood-based estimator defined through the exact forward map, but such an estimator is often computationally infeasible because it requires repeatedly solving the PDE. If the corresponding limiting variances are unequal, it would be interesting to understand whether this gap reflects a genuine computational-information-theoretic tradeoff, or whether there exists a computationally feasible procedure that also attains the semiparametric efficiency bound. Second, our theory focuses on finite-dimensional parameters, whereas many scientific inverse problems involve nonparametric or infinite-dimensional unknowns (e.g., spatially varying thermal conductivity). Extending the proposed debiasing and posterior-correction framework to such infinite-dimensional targets is therefore an important direction. Third, another natural extension is to high-dimensional parameter regimes, where the number of candidate PDE terms or physical parameters may grow with the sample size. Developing debiased inference procedures that also perform variable selection would be especially relevant for PDE discovery, where the goal is not only to estimate parameters but also to identify the active terms in the governing equation.

S.1.1 (1 + 1) transport PDE 

We begin with the 1 + 1 transport equation 

u⋆t (x, t ) − θ⋆u⋆x(x, t ) = 0 , (x, t ) ∈ [0 , 1] 2.

Observe that this PDE is a special case of our general formulation in (2.1), with 

K0 = ∂t, K1 = ∂x, Ψθ(x, z ) = −θz. 

Data-generating process. To generate data from a model that satisfies the PDE exactly, we take the true solution to be 

u⋆(x, t ) = sin  2π(x + θ⋆t) + cos  π(x + θ⋆t), with θ⋆ = 2 .

It is immediate that u⋆t (x, t ) = 2 u⋆x(x, t ). We generate observations according to the following procedure: 1. First, we generate ( Xi, T i) i.i.d.

∼ Unif([0 , 1] 2), 1 ≤ i ≤ n, where we consider three sample sizes 

n ∈ { 500 , 3000 , 5000 }.2. Next, for 1 ≤ i ≤ n, we set Yi = u⋆(Xi, T i) + εi, where εi i.i.d.

∼ N (0 , σ 2). In our experiments, we take σ = 0 .5. Following Section 2, we define the PDE risk using the boundary-vanishing weight ω(x, t ) = x2(1 −

x)2t2(1 − t)2. Consequently, for a fixed u, the minimizer θ(u) of R(θ, u ) has the following closed form:

Accordingly, the plug-in estimator ˆθPI (resp. the PINN estimator ˆθPINN ) is obtained by replacing 

u with the first-stage estimator bu (resp. the PINN estimator buPINN ). In the implementation, the above integrals are approximated by a midpoint quadrature rule on a 300 × 300 grid over [0 , 1] 2.

Implementation of the first-stage estimators. For both the plug-in and PINN procedures, the solution u⋆ is approximated by a fully connected neural network with three hidden layers, width 64, scalar output, and RePU activation of order 3, namely σ(z) = ( z+)3. Since our methodology requires accurate evaluation of first- and second-order derivatives, all computations are carried out using automatic differentiation in double precision. The expectations with respect to the distribu-tion of ( X, T ) that appear in the Sobolev penalty (2.4) and in the definition of R(θ, u ) (for example, in (2.2) and others) are approximated numerically by Monte Carlo collocation, using 1024 fresh draws from Unif([0 , 1] 2) per epoch for the Sobolev penalty and 4000 per epoch for the profiled PDE penalty. For estimating ( buPINN , ˆθPINN ), we use λn = 10 −5 and eλn = 10 (see (2.6) for their definitions), as these choices provide a good approximation to u⋆ and its derivatives. For estimating 

bu via (2.4), we again set λn = 10 −5. Thus, the tuning parameters are kept fixed throughout the experiment. The neural networks are trained in pytorch using the Adam optimizer with learning 39 rate 10 −3, for at most 20 ,000 epochs. We further employ early stopping with patience 300, based on a validation set, to stabilize training and prevent overfitting. 

Construction of the debiased estimator. In the present transport model, the score admits a particularly simple closed form. Indeed,

In contrast, the proposed debiasing step substantially reduces the bias, and the distribution of

The bottom panel presents boxplots of the three estimators for each sample size; the dashed line marks θ⋆ = 2.

Monte Carlo design and summary measures. For each sample size n ∈ { 500 , 3000 , 5000 },we repeat the experiment over 100 independent Monte Carlo runs, and in each run we record ˆθPI ,ˆθPINN and ˆθdb . The results are displayed in Figure 5, one for each coordinate of θ. Each figure shows, in the top row, kernel density estimates of the √n-scaled errors √n(ˆθj − θ⋆j ) for the three estimators at n ∈ { 500 , 3000 , 5000 }, overlaid with the limiting Gaussian law of Theorem 3.8; and in the bottom row, boxplots of the three estimators across the three sample sizes. Both ˆθPI and ˆθPINN 

are biased downward in both coordinates, whereas the debiased estimator ˆθdb is approximately centered at θ⋆: the density of √n(ˆθdb − θ⋆) concentrates around the limiting law as n grows, and the median of ˆθdb agrees closely with θ⋆ at n = 3000 and n = 5000. Its 95% confidence intervals attain approximately nominal coverage at these sample sizes (between 95% and 98%), with mild under-coverage at n = 500 (around 90%), reflecting the larger first-stage error in the small-sample regime. 

# S.2 Some results on Sobolev spaces 

In this section, we present some results on the Sobolev function space. As before, we use the notation W β,p (Ω) to denote the set of functions f : Ω → R with finite Sobolev norms, where Ω ⊆ Rd, and we use the notation Ck,γ (Ω) to denote the set of H¨ older functions with index k + γ,where k is a non-negative integer and γ ∈ (0 , 1). We use the notation CK (Ω) to denote CK, 0(Ω), i.e., the set of all K times differentiable functions with uniformly bounded derivatives. In our theoretical results, we use some key results for Sobolev function spaces, which we describe below: 

Theorem S.2.1 (Sobolev embedding theorem) . Let Ω = (0 , 1) d and f ∈ W β,p (Ω) for some integer 

β ≥ 1. If β − d/p is not an integer, then 

∥f ∥CK,γ (Ω) ≤ C∥f ∥W β,p (Ω) , K =



β − dp



, γ = β − dp − K , 

for some constant C > 0 independent of f . If β − d/p is an integer, then: 

∥f ∥CK (Ω) ≤ C∥f ∥W β,p (Ω) , K = β − dp − 1 ,

for some constant C > 0 independent of f .Proof. For a detailed proof, see Theorem 9.9, Corollary 9.11, 9.13, and 9.15 of [18]. The embedding theorem is first proved for Ω = Rd (Theorem 9.9 and Corollary 9.13 of [18]), and then the results are extended to any Ω ⊆ Rd with smooth boundary (Corollary 9.15 of [18]) using the argument of extension operator (Theorem 9.7 of [18]). For this extension argument to work, we require Ω to satisfy some smoothness conditions (see Theorem 5 of Chapter 6 of [86]), which are satisfied by (0 , 1) d (Example 2 of Section 3.3 of [86]). 

Theorem S.2.2 (Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation) . Let Ω = (0 , 1) d, let β ∈ N, and let 0 ≤ j < β. Let 1 ≤ p0, p 1 ≤ ∞ , 1 ≤ r ≤ ∞ , and let θ ∈ [j/β, 1] satisfy 

1

r = jd + θ

 1

p1

− βd



+ 1 − θp0

. (S.2.1) 44 Then, for every multi-index α with |α| = j and every f ∈ W β,p 1 (Ω) ∩Lp0 (Ω) , there exists a constant 

C = C(d, β, j, p 0, p 1, r, θ, Ω) , such that 

∥∂αf ∥Lr (Ω) ≤ C∥f ∥θW β,p 1 (Ω) ∥f ∥1−θLp0 (Ω) . (S.2.2) 

as long as θ < 1. In particular, taking p0 = p1 = p, we obtain that if

then 

∥∂αf ∥Lr (Ω) ≤ C∥f ∥θW β,p (Ω) ∥f ∥1−θLp(Ω) . (S.2.4) 

If 1 < p < ∞ and β − j − d/p is a non-negative integer, then the endpoint θ = 1 is excluded, and the conclusion holds for θ ∈ [j/β, 1) .Proof. We first recall the full Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality on Rd [71]. If g ∈ W β,p 1 (Rd)∩Lp0 (Rd)and if θ satisfies (S.2.1), then 

∥∂αg∥Lr (Rd) ≤ C∥g∥θW β,p 1 (Rd)∥g∥1−θLp0 (Rd). (S.2.5) The usual endpoint restriction is needed in the critical Sobolev case; in particular, when r = ∞ and 

p1 < ∞, we use the non-critical case θ < 1. Since Ω = (0 , 1) d is a bounded Lipschitz domain, there exists an extension operator E (see Theorem 5 of Chapter 6 of [86]) such that, for every integer β

and every 1 ≤ p1 ≤ ∞ ,

∥Ef ∥W β,p 1 (Rd) ≤ C∥f ∥W β,p 1 (Ω) .

Moreover, the extension can be chosen so that 

∥Ef ∥Lp0 (Rd) ≤ C∥f ∥Lp0 (Ω) 

for every 1 ≤ p0 ≤ ∞ . Applying Equation (S.2.5) to g = Ef gives 

∥∂αf ∥Lr (Ω) ≤ ∥ ∂αEf ∥Lr (Rd) ≤ C∥Ef ∥θW β,p 1 (Rd)∥Ef ∥1−θLp0 (Rd).

Using the two extension bounds yields 

∥∂αf ∥Lr (Ω) ≤ C∥f ∥θW β,p 1 (Ω) ∥f ∥1−θLp0 (Ω) .

This proves (S.2.2). Finally, if p0 = p1 = p, then (S.2.1) becomes 1

which gives (S.2.3) and hence the special case (S.2.4). 

# S.3 Proof of Proposition 3.4 

Proof. Take u ∈ W β, 2(Ω , L ), the space of all Sobolev functions with Sobolev norm at most L.Therefore, and since β − 2τ > d (Assumption 1), by the Sobolev embedding theorem (Theorem S.2.1), ∥∂αu∥L∞ ≤ L′ for some constant L′ uniformly over ( u, α) for all α with |α| ≤ 2τ . Conse-quently, by Assumption 4, there exists a nonnegative continuous function θ 7 → c1(θ) such that: 

|(Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x)| = |Ψ( x, K 1(u)( x), θ )| ≤ c1(θ), ∀ x ∈ [0 , 1] d, u ∈ W β, 2(Ω , L ) .

45 as ∥K1j (u)∥∞ ≤ L′. Furthermore, by another application of Assumption 4, we also have: 

|∂θj (Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x)) | = |∂θj Ψ( x, K 1(u)( x), θ )| ≤ c2(θ)for some nonnegative continuous function c2(·). As a consequence, 

{K0u(x) + (Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x)} ∂θj (Ψ θ ◦ K1u)( x) ≤ c2(θ)( L′ + c1(θ)) ,

for all x ∈ Ω and u ∈ W β, 2(Ω , L ). Therefore, we can justify interchanging the derivative and the integral via the dominated convergence theorem (DCT) to obtain: (∇θR)( θ, u ) =

# S.8 Neural network based approximation on Sobolev space 

In this section, we present a theorem about the optimal approximation error of a function f ∈

W β, 2((0 , 1) d, L ) using a RePU( ν) neural network (where ν ≥ β) in terms of the number of pa-rameters. Our main proof idea is closely related to that of [7], where the authors established the approximation error for H¨ older smooth functions, i.e., Cβ (Ω , L ). The key differences being: i) our function class W β, 2 which is larger than Cβ , and ii) we use RePU( ν) activation function for some 

ν ≥ β to ensure our neural networks have finite W β, 2 norm. Throughout this section, Sobolev derivatives are understood in the weak sense. 

Theorem S.8.1 (Approximation of W ν, 2((0 , 1) d) functions by RePU( ν) networks) . Let d ≥ 1 and let ν ∈ N with ν ≥ 3. Let Ω = (0 , 1) d and suppose f ∈ W ν, 2(Ω) . Then given any K ∈ N, there exists a RePU( ν) neural network NK with depth( NK ) ≤ c1(1 + ⌈log 2 d⌉), width( NK ) ≤ c2(K + ν)d,

sparsity( NK ) ≤ c3(K + ν)d, such that: 

∥f − N K ∥W r, 2(Ω) ≤ Cν,d K−(ν−r) ∥f ∥W ν, 2(Ω) , r = 0 , 1, . . . , ν, (S.8.1) 

Here the constants c1, c 2, c 3, C ν,d depends on (ν, d ), but not on K.Proof. The proof proceeds in six steps. 

Step 1: Sobolev approximation by tensor-product splines. Let {BK,ν j }K+νj=1 denote the univariate B-spline basis of degree ν on (0 , 1) associated with the open uniform knot sequence of spacing h = 1 /K . Let

Step 4: Extension to multiple products: We now extend the previous construction to the product of the form f (x1, . . . , x d) = x1x2 · · · xd by composing Mult ν in a balanced binary tree. Let 

L := ⌈log 2 d⌉ and set D := 2 L. Pad the input with ones: ˜xi := 

(

so that Qdi=1 xi = QDi=1 ˜xi. Define the intermediate variables z(0)  

> i

:= ˜ xi for 1 ≤ i ≤ D and for each level ℓ = 0 , 1, . . . , L − 1, 

z(ℓ+1)

:= Mult ν

 z(ℓ)2j−1, z (ℓ)2j

, j = 1 , . . . , D/ 2ℓ+1 .

When composing the multiplier blocks across different levels of the tree, the linear readout produc-ing each intermediate product can be absorbed into the affine transformation of the next RePU layer. More precisely, each intermediate product z(ℓ) 

> j

is a linear readout of the RePU features pro-duced at level ℓ. When z(ℓ) 

> j

is used as an input to a multiplier at level ℓ + 1, every preactivation in the next multiplier is an affine function of z(ℓ) 

> j

and hence, after substitution, an affine function of the RePU features from level ℓ. Thus the linear readout map can be composed with the affine input map of the next RePU layer and need not be counted as a separate nonlinear layer. Therefore, the above recursive construction is realized as an ordinary feedforward RePU( ν) network, up to changing constants in the width and sparsity bounds. Since z(ℓ) 

> j

∈ [0 , 1] for all ℓ, j (products of numbers in [0 , 1] stay in [0 , 1]), the multiplier is always applied within its exactness domain. After 

L levels, we obtain a single scalar 

z(L)1 =

> D

Y

> i=1

˜xi =

> d

Y

> i=1

xi.

This construction uses exactly L = ⌈log 2 d⌉ multiplication stages (each stage applies Mult ν in parallel), hence the number of RePU( ν) nonlinear layers is 1 + L. At level ℓ, there are D/ 2ℓ+1 

copies of Mult ν in parallel; since each copy uses m = 4 ⌊ν/ 2⌋ hidden units, the RePU width at level ℓ is mD/ 2ℓ+1 and the maximum width occurs at ℓ = 0, namely mD/ 2 ≤ m2L−1 ≍ md .Thus Qdi=1 xi can be computed exactly on [0 , 1] d by composing the two-input RePU( ν) multi-plier in a balanced binary tree of depth ⌈log 2 d⌉, padding with ones to the next power of two. Using this balanced binary-tree construction with the one-hidden-layer RePU( ν) multiplier, the function f (x1, . . . , x d) = Qdi=1 xi on [0 , 1] d is computed exactly by a RePU( ν) network of depth 

⌈log 2 d⌉, maximum width O(ν d ), with O(ν d ) active (nonzero) parameters, whose input weights lie in {− 1, 0, 1}, hidden biases are bounded by 2 + ⌊ν/ 2⌋, and all output weights are bounded by a constant Cν depending only on ν.

Step 5: Computing all possible tensor products of spline basis: Starting from the input 

x = ( x1, . . . , x d), we first compute all univariate spline values {BK,ν j (xℓ) : 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ d, 1 ≤ j ≤ K+ν}.Then we use the balanced-tree multiplier construction of previous step to compute all tensor prod-ucts {Φj(x)}j∈[K+ν]d . These d collections of size ( K+ν) are then combined using the exact two-input RePU( ν) multiplier Mult ν in a balanced binary-tree fashion: at each merge level, the current feature blocks are paired and all their entrywise products (i.e. outer products) are computed in parallel. 86 Repeating this procedure for ⌈log 2 d⌉ merge levels yields, simultaneously and exactly, the full tensor product feature map {Φj(x)}j∈[K+ν]d . Consequently, the mapping from x to all ( K + ν)d tensor-product spline basis functions can be realized by a RePU( ν) network of depth at most 1 + ⌈log 2 d⌉,with the final multiplication layer dominating the width on the order of ⌊ν/ 2⌋(K + ν)d, and with the total number of active (nonzero) parameters also scaling on the order of ( K + ν)d, up to con-stants depending only on ν. Therefore, starting from x = ( x1, x 2, · · · , x d), the full tensor-product feature map {Φj(x)}j∈[K+ν]d can be computed exactly by a RePU( ν) neural network of depth at most 1 + ⌈log 2 d⌉, maximum width at most Cν,d (K + ν)d, and total number of active (nonzero) parameters at most C′ 

> ν,d

(K + ν)d, where Cν,d , C ′ 

> ν,d

> 0 depend only on ( ν, d ) (and not on K). 

Step 6: Final linear readout: Adding a final linear layer with weights {cj}j∈[K+ν]d produces the scalar-valued network output 

NK (x) = X

> j∈[K+ν]d

cj Φj(x) = QK f (x).

Combining this identity with the spline approximation bound from Step 1 yields (S.8.1). 

Remark 10 (Effect of lower smoothness β < ν ). If the target function satisfies only f ∈ W β, 2((0 , 1) d)

for some β < ν , then the network architecture and construction remain unchanged , while only the approximation statement is modified. Indeed, we may still choose spline degree ν and use the same RePU( ν) network to compute all univariate B-splines, their tensor-product basis functions, and the final linear combination. The only difference is in the spline approximation step: the spline (or quasi-interpolant) QK f now satisfies 

∥f − QK f ∥W r, 2((0 ,1) d) ≤ Cβ,ν,d K−(β−r) ∥f ∥W β, 2((0 ,1) d), 0 ≤ r ≤ β, 

so the approximation rate is governed by the actual smoothness β rather than the spline degree ν.In particular, taking ν > β does not improve the convergence order but only increases constants and network size, while the depth, width, and sparsity bounds derived for the RePU (ν) realization continue to hold, up to constants depending on (β, ν, d ).
